People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Mostly around art and work, I think… or at least that’s where people notice it and say something. Like my artist statements or client notes can start in one place—textures, materials—and then I’ll jump to budgets or what I feel are the hidden meanings in the piece, and it sort of makes sense in my head, but in meetings people tell me they can’t follow me.

Not exactly like the words disappear… it’s more that I’ll start with the right words and then I sort of veer off. A little, maybe, but I notice it most with art and work because that’s where I have to explain things clearly.

Like I’ll start talking about textures, then suddenly budgets, then what something “means” in a way that makes sense to me, but other people say they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t know if that sounds serious or if I’m explaining it right, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought I should check in.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with communicating clearly happen in all situations, or is it mostly when you're talking about your art or work?\n\nPatient: Mostly around art and work, I think… or at least that’s where people notice it and say something. Like my artist statements or client notes can start in one place—textures, materials—and then I’ll jump to budgets or what I feel are the hidden meanings in the piece, and it sort of makes sense in my head, but in meetings people tell me they can’t follow me.\n\nI don’t mean to make it sound dramatic. It’s not every single conversation, I don’t think. But it has happened enough in work situations that I started worrying maybe I’m not explaining myself clearly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble finding the right words when you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like the words disappear… it’s more that I’ll start with the right words and then I sort of veer off. Like in an artist statement or a client note, I’ll be talking about texture, and then suddenly I’m explaining the budget, and then I’m talking about some hidden meaning behind the piece.\n\nIn my head it feels connected, but people in meetings have told me they can’t really follow what I’m trying to explain. I don’t know if that counts as trouble finding words, but it’s definitely been making me worry I’m not communicating clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty with grammar or putting sentences together when you speak or write?\n\nPatient: I don’t think my grammar is the main problem, exactly. Like, I can put sentences together okay… at least I think so. It’s more that the sentences start going in different directions.\n\nIn my artist statements or client notes, I’ll begin with textures or materials, and then I’m suddenly talking about budgets, and then something that feels like a secret meaning in the work. In my head there’s a connection, but in meetings people keep saying they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. Does that make sense as an answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what other people are saying to you, or is it mainly that you have trouble expressing your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think I understand other people okay, mostly. It’s more on my end, trying to get my thoughts out in a way that makes sense to them.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in—my artist statements, client notes, and even things I say in meetings have started jumping around, like from textures to budgets to… meanings I’m seeing in the work. It feels connected when I’m saying it, but people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. I’m not sure if I’m describing that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're having trouble understanding what someone else is trying to tell you, even when they're speaking clearly?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. If someone is speaking clearly, I usually feel like I understand what they mean.\n\nI mean, I might ask them to repeat something if I’m distracted or trying to make sure I got it right, but I wouldn’t say I’m having trouble understanding them. It feels more like the problem is when I try to explain my own thoughts back. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this difficulty with communicating clearly happening when you talk about things outside of art and work, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but I notice it most with art and work because that’s where I have to explain things clearly.\n\nWith friends or family, I think I can usually talk normally. But if I’m trying to describe something I’m working on, or why a piece matters, I can start jumping around again—from the materials to practical stuff like budgets, and then to meanings I’m seeing in it. In my head it feels connected, but they’ll kind of pause or ask what I mean.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as outside work exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m an artist, and lately my artist statements and client notes—and even things I say in meetings—kind of jump around. Like I’ll start talking about textures, then suddenly budgets, then what something “means” in a way that makes sense to me, but other people say they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t know if that sounds serious or if I’m explaining it right, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought I should check in.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with communicating clearly happen in all situations, or is it mostly when you're talking about your art or work?\n\nPatient: Mostly around art and work, I think… or at least that’s where people notice it and say something. Like my artist statements or client notes can start in one place—textures, materials—and then I’ll jump to budgets or what I feel are the hidden meanings in the piece, and it sort of makes sense in my head, but in meetings people tell me they can’t follow me.\n\nI don’t mean to make it sound dramatic. It’s not every single conversation, I don’t think. But it has happened enough in work situations that I started worrying maybe I’m not explaining myself clearly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble finding the right words when you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like the words disappear… it’s more that I’ll start with the right words and then I sort of veer off. I don’t know if that counts as trouble finding words, but it’s definitely been making me worry I’m not communicating clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty with grammar or putting sentences together when you speak or write?\n\nPatient: I don’t think my grammar is the main problem, exactly. Yeah… sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it can feel like my thoughts are moving faster than I can get them out.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF80.1, F80.2, R47.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a little trouble communicating clearly, more than I’d like to admit. I’m an artist, and lately my artist statements and client notes—and even things I say in meetings—kind of jump around. Like I’ll start talking about textures, then suddenly budgets, then what something “means” in a way that makes sense to me, but other people say they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t know if that sounds serious or if I’m explaining it right, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought I should check in.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with communicating clearly happen in all situations, or is it mostly when you're talking about your art or work?\n\nPatient: Mostly around art and work, I think… or at least that’s where people notice it and say something. Like my artist statements or client notes can start in one place—textures, materials—and then I’ll jump to budgets or what I feel are the hidden meanings in the piece, and it sort of makes sense in my head, but in meetings people tell me they can’t follow me.\n\nI don’t mean to make it sound dramatic. It’s not every single conversation, I don’t think. But it has happened enough in work situations that I started worrying maybe I’m not explaining myself clearly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble finding the right words when you're trying to communicate?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like the words disappear… it’s more that I’ll start with the right words and then I sort of veer off. Like in an artist statement or a client note, I’ll be talking about texture, and then suddenly I’m explaining the budget, and then I’m talking about some hidden meaning behind the piece.\n\nIn my head it feels connected, but people in meetings have told me they can’t really follow what I’m trying to explain. I don’t know if that counts as trouble finding words, but it’s definitely been making me worry I’m not communicating clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty with grammar or putting sentences together when you speak or write?\n\nPatient: I don’t think my grammar is the main problem, exactly. Like, I can put sentences together okay… at least I think so. It’s more that the sentences start going in different directions.\n\nIn my artist statements or client notes, I’ll begin with textures or materials, and then I’m suddenly talking about budgets, and then something that feels like a secret meaning in the work. In my head there’s a connection, but in meetings people keep saying they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. Does that make sense as an answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what other people are saying to you, or is it mainly that you have trouble expressing your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think I understand other people okay, mostly. It’s more on my end, trying to get my thoughts out in a way that makes sense to them.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in—my artist statements, client notes, and even things I say in meetings have started jumping around, like from textures to budgets to… meanings I’m seeing in the work. It feels connected when I’m saying it, but people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. I’m not sure if I’m describing that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're having trouble understanding what someone else is trying to tell you, even when they're speaking clearly?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. If someone is speaking clearly, I usually feel like I understand what they mean.\n\nI mean, I might ask them to repeat something if I’m distracted or trying to make sure I got it right, but I wouldn’t say I’m having trouble understanding them. It feels more like the problem is when I try to explain my own thoughts back. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this difficulty with communicating clearly happening when you talk about things outside of art and work, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but I notice it most with art and work because that’s where I have to explain things clearly.\n\nWith friends or family, I think I can usually talk normally. But if I’m trying to describe something I’m working on, or why a piece matters, I can start jumping around again—from the materials to practical stuff like budgets, and then to meanings I’m seeing in it. In my head it feels connected, but they’ll kind of pause or ask what I mean.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as outside work exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this difficulty with explaining your thoughts clearly happens when you're discussing specific topics, like art, or if it can happen with any topic?\n\nPatient: Mostly with art and work, I think. That’s where it shows up enough that people notice.\n\nLike, my artist statements or client notes, or even when I’m talking in meetings, I’ll start with textures or materials, then suddenly I’m talking about budgets, and then… the meanings I’m seeing in the work. It feels connected to me while I’m saying it, but people keep telling me they can’t really follow what I’m trying to explain.\n\nI don’t think it happens with every single topic, or at least not as much. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any difficulties with speech, language, or communication when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I did okay in school and I don’t remember anyone saying I had speech or language problems as a kid.\n\nMaybe I was a little shy sometimes, but I think that was more just personality, not that I couldn’t communicate. My parents never told me there was anything like that, at least. Is that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological or developmental disorders?\n\nPatient: No, not that I’m aware of. I’ve never been told I had anything neurological or developmental.\n\nI mean, I haven’t had any kind of diagnosis like that, at least. If there’s something specific you mean, I can try to answer, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or moving quickly when you're having trouble explaining something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it can feel like my thoughts are moving faster than I can get them out.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to explain one idea, and then images, possible titles, grant language, colors, something someone said earlier—all of it kind of stacks up before I’ve finished the first sentence. In my head it feels like it’s all connected, but when I say it out loud, I think it comes out jumbled.\n\nIs that what you mean by racing thoughts?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know if that counts as trouble finding words, but it’s definitely been making me worry I’m not communicating clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty with grammar or putting sentences together when you speak or write?\n\nPatient: I don’t think my grammar is the main problem, exactly. If someone is speaking clearly, I usually feel like I understand what they mean.\n\nI mean, I might ask them to repeat something if I’m distracted or trying to make sure I got it right, but I wouldn’t say I’m having trouble understanding them. It feels more like the problem is when I try to explain my own thoughts back. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this difficulty with communicating clearly happening when you talk about things outside of art and work, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but I notice it most with art and work because that’s where I have to explain things clearly.\n\nWith friends or family, I think I can usually talk normally. But if I’m trying to describe something I’m working on, or why a piece matters, I can start jumping around again—from the materials to practical stuff like budgets, and then to meanings I’m seeing in it. In my head it feels connected, but they’ll kind of pause or ask what I mean.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as outside work exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this difficulty with explaining your thoughts clearly happens when you're discussing specific topics, like art, or if it can happen with any topic?\n\nPatient: Mostly with art and work, I think. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any difficulties with speech, language, or communication when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. At least, not that I know of. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to communicate in writing or in spoken language?\n\nPatient: Both, I think… but maybe writing makes it more obvious, because people can look at it and say, “I don’t understand what you mean here.”\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I’m not sure it would be about the language, exactly. I mean, maybe speaking another language would make it harder in a normal way, like finding words… but what I’m talking about feels like it happens underneath that.

In my head there’s a connection, but in meetings people keep saying they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. Does that make sense as an answer?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what other people are saying to you, or is it mainly that you have trouble expressing your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: I think I understand other people okay, mostly. It’s more on my end, trying to get my thoughts out in a way that makes sense to them.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in—my artist statements, client notes, and even things I say in meetings have started jumping around, like from textures to budgets to… meanings I’m seeing in the work. It feels connected when I’m saying it, but people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain. I’m not sure if I’m describing that right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're having trouble understanding what someone else is trying to tell you, even when they're speaking clearly?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so. If someone is speaking clearly, I usually feel like I understand what they mean.\n\nI mean, I might ask them to repeat something if I’m distracted or trying to make sure I got it right, but I wouldn’t say I’m having trouble understanding them. It feels more like the problem is when I try to explain my own thoughts back. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice this difficulty with communicating clearly happening when you talk about things outside of art and work, like with friends or family?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but I notice it most with art and work because that’s where I have to explain things clearly.\n\nWith friends or family, I think I can usually talk normally. But if I’m trying to describe something I’m working on, or why a piece matters, I can start jumping around again—from the materials to practical stuff like budgets, and then to meanings I’m seeing in it. In my head it feels connected, but they’ll kind of pause or ask what I mean.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as outside work exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this difficulty with explaining your thoughts clearly happens when you're discussing specific topics, like art, or if it can happen with any topic?\n\nPatient: Mostly with art and work, I think. That’s where it shows up enough that people notice.\n\nLike, my artist statements or client notes, or even when I’m talking in meetings, I’ll start with textures or materials, then suddenly I’m talking about budgets, and then… the meanings I’m seeing in the work. It feels connected to me while I’m saying it, but people keep telling me they can’t really follow what I’m trying to explain.\n\nI don’t think it happens with every single topic, or at least not as much. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any difficulties with speech, language, or communication when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I did okay in school and I don’t remember anyone saying I had speech or language problems as a kid.\n\nMaybe I was a little shy sometimes, but I think that was more just personality, not that I couldn’t communicate. My parents never told me there was anything like that, at least. Is that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological or developmental disorders?\n\nPatient: No, not that I’m aware of. I’ve never been told I had anything neurological or developmental.\n\nI mean, I haven’t had any kind of diagnosis like that, at least. If there’s something specific you mean, I can try to answer, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or moving quickly when you're having trouble explaining something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it can feel like my thoughts are moving faster than I can get them out.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to explain one idea, and then images, possible titles, grant language, colors, something someone said earlier—all of it kind of stacks up before I’ve finished the first sentence. In my head it feels like it’s all connected, but when I say it out loud, I think it comes out jumbled.\n\nIs that what you mean by racing thoughts?\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have difficulty with reading, writing, or learning new information in school?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I got through school pretty normally and I finished college, so I don’t remember having a specific problem with reading or writing or learning things.\n\nThere were subjects I had to work harder at, like anyone, but nothing where I was told I had a learning issue. At least, not that I know of. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to communicate in writing or in spoken language?\n\nPatient: Both, I think… but maybe writing makes it more obvious, because people can look at it and say, “I don’t understand what you mean here.”\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. My artist statements and client notes have started kind of jumping around—from textures to budgets to, um, meanings I’m trying to explain—and then in meetings I’ll do it out loud too. I think I’m making a clear connection, but people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nI don’t know if that’s a communication problem exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s embarrassing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your trouble with communicating clearly happens more often when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it probably does get worse when I’m stressed or feeling put on the spot… though it’s not only then.\n\nLike, part of why I came in is that my artist statements and client notes—and even things I say in meetings—have started jumping around. I’ll go from textures to budgets to what feels like hidden or, um, secret meanings I’m trying to explain, and people tell me they can’t follow me.\n\nI don’t mean to sound dramatic. In my head it feels connected, but I can tell it’s not coming out clearly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with communicating clearly is related to how you're feeling at the time, like if you're excited, nervous, or calm?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is related, at least somewhat. When I’m excited or nervous, it’s like my thoughts move faster and I try to explain too many connections at once.\n\nThat’s what I mean with the artist statements and client notes, or even in meetings—I’ll start with textures, then somehow I’m talking about budgets and then the meanings behind things, and it makes sense to me, but people say they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been happening enough that I noticed. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same trouble communicating your ideas if you were speaking a different language?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would be about the language, exactly. I mean, maybe speaking another language would make it harder in a normal way, like finding words… but what I’m talking about feels like it happens underneath that.\n\nEven in English, with my artist statements or client notes, I’ll start trying to explain something about texture and then I’m suddenly talking about budgets or these meanings I think are important, and people say they can’t follow the jump. So… I think it might still happen in another language too, just maybe look different?\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that your trouble with communicating clearly is worse when you're feeling a certain way, such as anxious or upset?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my artist statements, client notes, and even what I say in meetings have started jumping from textures to budgets to secret meanings, and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it’s worse when I’m anxious or upset… or even just feeling pressured to explain myself well.

I don’t think it’s only that, though. The main thing I noticed—and I guess why I came in—is that my artist statements and client notes, and even things I say in meetings, have started to kind of jump around. But if I’m trying to describe something I’m working on, or why a piece matters, I can start jumping around again—from the materials to practical stuff like budgets, and then to meanings I’m seeing in it. In my head it feels connected, but they’ll kind of pause or ask what I mean.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as outside work exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this difficulty with explaining your thoughts clearly happens when you're discussing specific topics, like art, or if it can happen with any topic?\n\nPatient: Mostly with art and work, I think. That’s where it shows up enough that people notice.\n\nLike, my artist statements or client notes, or even when I’m talking in meetings, I’ll start with textures or materials, then suddenly I’m talking about budgets, and then… the meanings I’m seeing in the work. It feels connected to me while I’m saying it, but people keep telling me they can’t really follow what I’m trying to explain.\n\nI don’t think it happens with every single topic, or at least not as much. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any difficulties with speech, language, or communication when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I did okay in school and I don’t remember anyone saying I had speech or language problems as a kid.\n\nMaybe I was a little shy sometimes, but I think that was more just personality, not that I couldn’t communicate. My parents never told me there was anything like that, at least. Is that the kind of history you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with any neurological or developmental disorders?\n\nPatient: No, not that I’m aware of. I’ve never been told I had anything neurological or developmental.\n\nI mean, I haven’t had any kind of diagnosis like that, at least. If there’s something specific you mean, I can try to answer, but I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or moving quickly when you're having trouble explaining something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it can feel like my thoughts are moving faster than I can get them out.\n\nLike, I’ll be trying to explain one idea, and then images, possible titles, grant language, colors, something someone said earlier—all of it kind of stacks up before I’ve finished the first sentence. In my head it feels like it’s all connected, but when I say it out loud, I think it comes out jumbled.\n\nIs that what you mean by racing thoughts?\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have difficulty with reading, writing, or learning new information in school?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I got through school pretty normally and I finished college, so I don’t remember having a specific problem with reading or writing or learning things.\n\nThere were subjects I had to work harder at, like anyone, but nothing where I was told I had a learning issue. At least, not that I know of. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to communicate in writing or in spoken language?\n\nPatient: Both, I think… but maybe writing makes it more obvious, because people can look at it and say, “I don’t understand what you mean here.”\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. My artist statements and client notes have started kind of jumping around—from textures to budgets to, um, meanings I’m trying to explain—and then in meetings I’ll do it out loud too. I think I’m making a clear connection, but people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nI don’t know if that’s a communication problem exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s embarrassing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that your trouble with communicating clearly happens more often when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it probably does get worse when I’m stressed or feeling put on the spot… though it’s not only then.\n\nLike, part of why I came in is that my artist statements and client notes—and even things I say in meetings—have started jumping around. I’ll go from textures to budgets to what feels like hidden or, um, secret meanings I’m trying to explain, and people tell me they can’t follow me.\n\nI don’t mean to sound dramatic. In my head it feels connected, but I can tell it’s not coming out clearly. In my head it feels connected, but I can tell it’s not coming out clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this trouble with communicating clearly getting better, worse, or staying about the same over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s gotten a little worse over time… not dramatically, I don’t want to overstate it, but enough that I finally felt like I should ask someone.\n\nIt used to be more occasional, maybe only when I was trying to explain an artwork. But lately it’s been showing up in my artist statements, client notes, and even meetings. I’ll start with textures, then somehow go to budgets, then these secret meanings or connections, and people keep saying they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain.\n\nSo… worse, I guess. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with communication start during childhood or have they developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: More recently, I think. I don’t remember this being a childhood thing, at least not in the same way.\n\nIt’s really something I’ve noticed lately because my artist statements, client notes, and even what I say in meetings have started kind of… jumping around. Like I’ll mean to talk about textures, then I’m suddenly bringing up budgets, and then these secret meanings or connections, and people tell me they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining that right, but it feels more like it developed recently than something that’s always been there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your trouble with communicating clearly would improve if you were given more time to gather your thoughts before speaking or writing?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / artist_or_related_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my artist statements, client notes, and even what I say in meetings have started jumping from textures to budgets to secret meanings, and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little, yes. If I have more time, I can sometimes make it sound more organized.

But… I don’t think it completely fixes it. Even when I’m writing, like with artist statements or client notes, I’ll think I’m being clear and then it still kind of jumps—from textures to budgets to these meanings or connections I’m trying to explain. And then people tell me they can’t follow what I mean.

So time helps somewhat, I guess, but it doesn’t make it go away. Is that… does that answer it?

Like I’ll start talking about textures, then suddenly budgets, then what something “means” in a way that makes sense to me, but other people say they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t know if that sounds serious or if I’m explaining it right, but it’s been coming up enough that I thought I should check in.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with communicating clearly happen in all situations, or is it mostly when you're talking about your art or work?\n\nPatient: Mostly around art and work, I think… or at least that’s where people notice it and say something. My parents never told me there was anything like that, at least. In my head it feels like it’s all connected, but when I say it out loud, I think it comes out jumbled.\n\nIs that what you mean by racing thoughts?\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have difficulty with reading, writing, or learning new information in school?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I got through school pretty normally and I finished college, so I don’t remember having a specific problem with reading or writing or learning things.\n\nThere were subjects I had to work harder at, like anyone, but nothing where I was told I had a learning issue. At least, not that I know of. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it harder to communicate in writing or in spoken language?\n\nPatient: Both, I think… but maybe writing makes it more obvious, because people can look at it and say, “I don’t understand what you mean here.”\n\nThat’s actually part of why I came in. I think I’m making a clear connection, but people keep telling me they can’t follow what I’m trying to say.\n\nI don’t know if that’s a communication problem exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s embarrassing. In my head it feels connected, but I can tell it’s not coming out clearly. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same trouble communicating your ideas if you were speaking a different language?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it would be about the language, exactly. So… I think it might still happen in another language too, just maybe look different?\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your trouble with communicating clearly is worse when you're feeling a certain way, such as anxious or upset?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it’s worse when I’m anxious or upset… or even just feeling pressured to explain myself well.\n\nI don’t think it’s only that, though. The main thing I noticed—and I guess why I came in—is that my artist statements and client notes, and even things I say in meetings, have started to kind of jump around. I’ll be talking about textures, then I’m suddenly on budgets, and then these secret meanings or connections I’m trying to explain, and people keep telling me they can’t follow what I mean.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little strange. In my head it feels connected, but I can tell it’s not coming out clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this trouble with communicating clearly getting better, worse, or staying about the same over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s gotten a little worse over time… not dramatically, I don’t want to overstate it, but enough that I finally felt like I should ask someone.\n\nIt used to be more occasional, maybe only when I was trying to explain an artwork. But lately it’s been showing up in my artist statements, client notes, and even meetings. I’ll start with textures, then somehow go to budgets, then these secret meanings or connections, and people keep saying they can’t follow what I’m trying to explain.\n\nSo… worse, I guess. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with communication start during childhood or have they developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: More recently, I think. I don’t remember this being a childhood thing, at least not in the same way.\n\nIt’s really something I’ve noticed lately because my artist statements, client notes, and even what I say in meetings have started kind of… jumping around. Like I’ll mean to talk about textures, then I’m suddenly bringing up budgets, and then these secret meanings or connections, and people tell me they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t know if I’m explaining that right, but it feels more like it developed recently than something that’s always been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with communicating clearly would improve if you were given more time to gather your thoughts before speaking or writing?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. If I have more time, I can sometimes make it sound more organized.\n\nBut… I don’t think it completely fixes it. Even when I’m writing, like with artist statements or client notes, I’ll think I’m being clear and then it still kind of jumps—from textures to budgets to these meanings or connections I’m trying to explain. And then people tell me they can’t follow what I mean.\n\nSo time helps somewhat, I guess, but it doesn’t make it go away. Is that… does that answer it?"
